## Reasoning

**Mission Analysis:**
- Score increases by 1 per robot outside the target region (BLUE-walled strip) per step
- Target region has BLACK floor (strips at both ends have black floors, but only the BLUE-walled one is the target)
- Wait — both strips have BLACK floors. The middle is GRAY. So floor color alone can't distinguish target from non-target strip.
- Robots need to find the BLUE walls (condition C=7 with colour blue=3) to identify the target strip, then stay there.

**Key perceptions:**
- Floor BLACK = in a strip (either target or non-target)
- Floor GRAY = in the middle corridor
- Perceiving BLUE color = near/facing target strip walls
- Perceiving GREEN color = near/facing wrong strip walls

**Strategy:**
- Robots should move toward BLUE walls (GoToColor with clr=3)
- Once in the black floor zone AND seeing blue, stay there (Stop or slow movement)
- Avoid the green-walled strip

**State designs:**

**Simple 2-state:** 
- S0: GoToColor (blue), if floor is BLACK → S1
- S1: Exploration (slow), if floor GRAY → S0, if perceive GREEN → S0

**3-state approach:**
- S0: Exploration (find blue walls)
- S1: GoToColor blue (approach target)  
- S2: Stop (in target region - black floor + blue visible)

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
I need to see the results from Round 2 to know which machines performed best. Since no scores were provided, I'll reason from first principles and diversify my designs.

## Reasoning

**Mission recap:** Robots must reach the BLUE-walled strip (BLACK floor). Both strips have BLACK floors; middle is GRAY. Key: use C=7 (perceive color) with blue (color 3) to find target, avoid green (color 2).

**Core logic:**
- GoToColor(blue) navigates toward target strip walls
- When floor is BLACK AND no green visible → likely in target → Stop or slow exploration
- When floor is GRAY → navigate toward blue
- GoAwayColor(green) helps escape wrong strip

**Key insight I may have missed:** The `--n<I>x<K>` destination is indexed among OTHER states. With 2 states, leaving state 0, destination 0 = state 1. With 3 states leaving state 0, destination 0 = state 1, destination 1 = state 2. Let me be careful here.

**Strategy:**
- Best simple design: S0=GoToColor(blue), transition on black floor → S1=Stop, S1 transitions back on gray floor
- Add escape from green strip: GoAwayColor(green) when perceiving green

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

I still haven't received score feedback, which means I need to continue exploring systematically. Let me reconsider the problem more carefully.

**Critical re-analysis:**
- Both strips have BLACK floors; the BLUE-walled strip is target
- Robots start in the GRAY middle area
- GoToColor(blue=3) should steer robots toward blue walls = target strip
- GoAwayColor(green=2) should push robots away from the wrong strip
- Once on BLACK floor with blue walls visible → Stop is ideal

**Key concern:** When a robot is IN the blue-walled strip and facing inward, it may NOT see the blue walls (they're behind it). So "stop when floor is black" might be better than "stop when floor is black AND see blue."

**Also:** The destination indexing. With 2 states, leaving state 0, D=0 means state 1 (the only other state). Leaving state 1, D=0 means state 0. This is correct in my designs.

**Best hypothesis:** Simple 2-state: GoToColor(blue) → [black floor] → Stop → [gray floor] → GoToColor(blue). The stop state keeps robots in target. Add small random escape probability to avoid getting stuck on wrong black strip.

**New ideas:**
- Use GoAwayColor(green) as primary behavior when green is visible
- Use attraction to aggregate once in target zone
- Try without Stop: just GoToColor(blue) always, with floor-based modulation

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

I've been running for 4 rounds without score feedback. Let me reconsider fundamentally.

**Re-reading the mission:** "AGGREGATION mission" - but the scoring says robots outside the TARGET REGION (blue-walled strip) score points. So this is actually a **foraging/homing** task: get all robots to the blue strip and keep them there.

**Critical insight I may have been wrong about:**
- The mission title says "AGGREGATION" but the score is about being in the target region
- Wait - re-reading: "score increases by 1 for every robot that is outside the target region" - this IS about getting robots to the blue strip

**Floor colors:** BLACK in both strips, GRAY in middle. So I cannot use floor color alone to distinguish the two strips. I need wall color perception.

**What colors are available for C=7?**
- Color 1=red, 2=green, 3=blue, 4=yellow, 5=magenta, 6=cyan
- Blue walls = color 3, Green walls = color 2

**Best strategy:**
1. If seeing GREEN walls → run away (GoAwayColor green)
2. If seeing BLUE walls → go toward them (GoToColor blue)  
3. If on BLACK floor (in a strip) and NOT seeing green → stay (Stop)
4. If on GRAY floor → seek blue

**Problem with GoToColor(blue):** Once inside the blue strip, the robot faces the wall and may see blue — it should stop, not keep going into the wall.

**Better design:** 
- S0: GoToColor(blue) — approach target
- Transition: black floor → S1 (we're in a strip)
- S1: Stop — stay put
- Transition: gray floor → S0 (left the strip, go back)
- Transition: see green → S2 (wrong strip!)
- S2: GoAwayColor(green) — escape wrong strip
- Transition: gray floor → S0

The simplest robust design uses just 2 states with the stop-on-black logic. Let me also try variants where the "stop" state is replaced by slow exploration to help robots spread within the target zone.

**Also trying:** Using only GoToColor(blue) with no stop — robot always chases blue walls, which keeps it near the blue strip boundary. Not ideal for staying inside.

**New insight:** Maybe I should try GoAwayColor(green) as the primary behavior - robots flee green walls and naturally end up in the blue strip.

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

After 5 rounds without score feedback, I need to think more carefully about what might be going wrong with my designs.

**Fundamental re-examination:**

The mission is called "AGGREGATION" but the scoring is about being in the target region (blue-walled strip). The target strip has BLACK floor and BLUE walls. The wrong strip has BLACK floor and GREEN walls. Middle is GRAY.

**What I think is happening:** My designs may be working but the optimizer hasn't found the best parameters yet. Let me think about failure modes:

1. **GoToColor(blue)** - robots move toward blue walls. When they reach the blue strip, they hit the wall. This is actually fine - they're IN the target region while pressing against the wall.

2. **Stop on black floor** - once in ANY black strip, robots stop. But some robots may stop in the GREEN strip! This is bad.

3. **Key fix:** In the stop state, I need a transition that fires when GREEN is visible → escape. Currently my best designs may not handle robots stuck in the wrong (green) strip.

**Revised best design:**
- S0: GoToColor(blue) → if black floor: S1
- S1: Stop → if gray floor: S0 (fell back), if see green: S2 (wrong strip!)
- S2: GoAwayColor(green) → if gray floor: S0, if black floor & no green: S1

**Also critical:** Color perception. Blue=3 in the color coding. Let me verify: clr values 1-6 correspond to... the problem says `--clr<I> one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}`. And for C=7, `--l<I>x<K> one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}`. The wall colors are BLUE and GREEN. In ARGoS, typical color encoding: 1=red, 2=green, 3=blue, 4=yellow, 5=magenta, 6=cyan. So GREEN walls = color 2, BLUE walls = color 3. This seems right.

**Most promising topology:** 3 states with explicit green-escape logic.

FSM: --nstates 3 --s0 8 --vel0 1.0 --clr0 3 --cle0 0 --n0 1 --n0x0 0 --c0x0 0 --p0x0 0.9 --s1 1 --cle1 0 --n1 2 --n1x0 0 --c1x0 1 --p1x0 0.9 --n1x1 2 --c1x1 7 --l1x1 2 --p1x1 0.9 --s2 9 --vel2 1.0 --clr2 2 --cle2 0 --n2 2 --n2x0 0 --c2x0 1 --p2x0 0.9 --n2x1 1 --c2x1 0 --p2x1 0.85

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

After 6 rounds with no score feedback provided to me, I need to radically rethink my approach. The optimizer is running but I don't know which machines are winning. Let me reason from scratch about what would truly work.

**Core problem:** Get robots to the BLUE-walled strip (BLACK floor) and keep them there. The challenge: both strips have BLACK floors, so floor=black alone doesn't identify the target.

**What sensors can distinguish the two strips:**
- C=7 with color 3 (blue): visible only when near/facing blue walls = target strip
- C=7 with color 2 (green): visible only when near/facing green walls = wrong strip

**Behavioral logic:**
1. A robot in the GRAY middle should seek blue → GoToColor(blue)
2. A robot seeing GREEN is near the wrong strip → GoAwayColor(green)  
3. A robot on BLACK floor without seeing green → probably in right strip → Stop or slow move

**Key insight I've been missing:** When a robot is INSIDE the blue strip, it may be surrounded by blue walls on one side but facing inward. The C=7 condition fires if ANY camera sees the color. So inside the blue strip, robots WILL see blue walls. Inside the green strip, they WILL see green walls. This is the distinguishing signal!

**Therefore:**
- If seeing BLUE → we're near/in target → stop or stay
- If seeing GREEN → escape with GoAwayColor(green)
- If seeing NEITHER (gray middle) → GoToColor(blue) to find target

**Best 3-state design:**
- S0: GoToColor(blue) [default search] → see blue + black floor → S1; see green → S2
- S1: Stop [in target] → gray floor → S0; see green → S2
- S2: GoAwayColor(green) [escape wrong strip] → gray floor → S0; see blue → S1

**Also trying:** Pure GoAwayColor(green) - robots flee green walls and naturally cluster in blue strip. Simple but might work.

**Also trying:** Stop as default with transitions to move only when needed.

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

Seven rounds in with no score feedback. I need to think about what's fundamentally different I could try.

**Critical re-examination of the problem:**

The mission says "AGGREGATION" - but the score is about robots being outside the target region. Let me re-read: "score increases by 1 for every robot outside the target region." So minimizing = get all 20 robots into the blue strip ASAP and keep them there for all 1200 steps.

**What I may have been getting wrong:**

1. **GoToColor(blue)** behavior: the robot moves toward the nearest blue object. If the robot is IN the blue strip already, it sees blue walls all around and may spin or behave erratically trying to "go toward" a wall it's already at. This could cause robots to leave the strip.

2. **Stop on black floor**: If a robot stops in the GREEN strip (black floor too!), it scores badly. The transition "see green → escape" is critical but may not fire reliably if the robot just entered the wrong strip and is facing inward.

3. **The C=7 condition** fires when the robot's camera perceives the color. Inside a strip, the robot WILL see the bounding walls. So a robot deep inside the blue strip will see blue walls → C=7(blue) fires. A robot deep in the green strip will see green walls.

**What might actually work better:**

A robot that:
- Primarily flees GREEN walls (GoAwayColor green) - this pushes it away from the wrong strip
- When it can't see green (i.e., it's in the blue strip or far from green walls) → Stop
- This is elegant: flee green, stop when green is gone

**2-state design:**
- S0: GoAwayColor(green) → if NOT seeing green (no C=7 green firing) → stop
- But C=7 is a positive condition (fires when color IS seen), not negative

**Alternative using probability:**
- S0: GoAwayColor(green), transition on C=0 (black floor) with high prob → S1
- S1: Stop, transition on C=7(green) → S0 (escape green strip)

**Another approach:** Use GoToColor(blue) as the only behavior - robots always chase blue walls. They'll press against the blue walls, which means they're IN the target region. The issue is they oscillate at the boundary, but that's still better than being in the gray middle.

**Pure GoToColor(blue) with NO stop:** Robots always move toward blue. Once at the blue wall, they keep pressing against it. They're in the target region. Score might be decent.

**Let me try radically different topologies:**

1. Single state: GoToColor(blue) - simplest possible, always chasing blue
2. Single state: GoAwayColor(green) - always fleeing green  
3. Two states: GoAwayColor(green) → [black floor, no green visible] → Stop → [see green] → GoAwayColor(green)
4. Two states: GoToColor(blue) → [see blue] → Stop → [gray floor] → GoToColor(blue)

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

Eight rounds without score feedback. I need to be very systematic now with only 2 rounds left after this.

**Fundamental rethink - what behaviors actually make physical sense:**

The robots start in the GRAY middle. They need to reach the BLUE-walled strip.

**Most physically logical approach:**
- GoToColor(blue=3): robot moves toward blue objects in camera. Blue walls are at the target strip boundary. Robot will naturally navigate toward and into the target strip.
- Once there, Stop keeps them in place.
- The challenge: distinguishing black floor of target vs black floor of wrong strip.

**But wait - GoToColor moves toward the nearest blue OBJECT.** If the robot is already pressed against the blue wall inside the target strip, it might see blue very close and "go toward" it by turning and bumping. Or it might be stable. 

**GoAwayColor(green=2):** Robot moves away from green objects. Green walls are at the wrong strip. This naturally pushes robots toward the blue strip side. A robot in the gray area that sees green walls will move away = toward blue side. A robot in the blue strip won't see green walls (they're far away, on opposite end) so this behavior becomes neutral/random there.

**Key insight:** GoAwayColor(green) is actually excellent because:
1. In gray middle: pushes robots toward blue end ✓
2. In blue strip: green walls are ~full arena away, likely not visible, so robot wanders within blue strip ✓  
3. In green strip: pushes robot OUT of wrong strip ✓

**But does the camera have enough range?** The range-and-bearing is 0.5m but camera range for color isn't specified - it's likely the full visual field. In ARGoS, the camera can see walls at arena-scale distances.

**Pure GoAwayColor(green) single state** might be the best simple solution. Let me prioritize this.

**Also try:** GoAwayColor(green) with Stop when in black floor AND NOT seeing green.

**Another key insight:** Maybe I should use `--n0 0` (zero transitions = stay forever in state) for single-state machines.

Let me now design 13 machines focused on the most promising approaches:

1. Single state GoAwayColor(green) - pure

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

This is the final round. Nine rounds of exploration without score feedback. I need to submit my best 12 machines.

**What I know with confidence:**
- Target = BLUE-walled strip (BLACK floor)
- Wrong strip = GREEN-walled strip (BLACK floor)  
- Middle = GRAY floor
- Key sensors: C=7(color 3=blue walls), C=7(color 2=green walls), C=0(black floor), C=1(gray floor)

**Best behavioral logic I've converged on:**

The most physically sensible designs:

1. **GoAwayColor(green) → Stop**: Flee green walls, stop when on black floor without green. This handles all three zones correctly.

2. **GoToColor(blue) → Stop**: Navigate to blue walls, stop when there.

3. **The "see green = wrong strip" logic**: If a robot sees green walls, it's near the wrong strip. GoAwayColor(green) pushes it out. If it doesn't see green and is on black floor = likely in blue strip → Stop.

4. **Single state GoAwayColor(green)**: Always flee green. In blue strip, green walls are far/invisible so robot wanders within strip. Simple and robust.

**For the final round, I'll focus on:**
- The strongest single-state designs (always flee green OR always seek blue)
- The best 2-state designs (flee green → stop on black; seek blue → stop on black)
- The best 3-state design with explicit green-escape
- Variants with tight probabilities (0.95-0.99) to make transitions more reliable

**Key parameter insight:** High transition probabilities (0.95-0.99) make transitions fire more reliably when conditions are met, reducing time spent in wrong states.

################################################################################
# PROMPT SYSTEME
################################################################################

You are an expert in swarm robotics working with the ARGoS3 simulator and the AutoMoDe framework.
You act as the variation operator of an evolutionary algorithm: you read simulation feedback and you emit valid AutoMoDe finite-state-machine command lines.

# 1. THE MISSION
The score is MINIMISED.

At every step, the score increases by 1 for every robot that is outside the target region. The score does not depend on how close the robots are to each other.

The arena has two strips at opposite ends. The floor is BLACK in both strips and GRAY in between. The target region is the strip whose bordering walls are BLUE; the walls bordering the other strip are GREEN.

# 2. THE ROBOTS AND THE ARENA
The robots operate inside a closed arena bounded by walls. 20 e-puck robots start scattered around the centre of the arena, with random positions and orientations that change at every run. Each robot perceives its neighbours through a range-and-bearing device with a range of 0.50 m: neighbour-count conditions only count robots closer than that. A run lasts 1200 simulation steps (120 s at 10 steps per second).

# 3. HOW TO APPROACH THE DESIGN
These are general principles, valid for any mission. They are not a solution to this one.
- Derive the target behaviour from the scoring rule: for each situation a robot can be in, decide whether the score rewards or penalises it.
- Identify what the robots can perceive - floor colour under them, coloured objects in view, neighbours within range - and which of these perceptions carry information about the situations the score cares about.
- Use one state per distinct activity, and trigger transitions on the perceptions that indicate it is time to change activity.

# 4. HOW TO ANSWER
First write your reasoning in plain language: how many states, what each state does physically, and what triggers each transition. Two or three sentences per machine is enough.
Then write each finite state machine on its own line, starting exactly with "FSM: " and nothing before it.

# 5. COMMAND-LINE FORMAT
General shape:
  --nstates N  --s<I> B [behaviour params] --cle<I> L  --n<I> T  [--n<I>x<K> D --c<I>x<K> C <condition params>]
<I> is the state index, <K> the index of a transition leaving that state. Both start at 0.

## A. Global
* `--nstates N` : number of states, integer in [1..4].

## B. Behaviours — use ONLY these ids
  B=0  Exploration   -> moves forward and turns randomly
            requires --rwm<I>  integer in [1..100]  (turning rate)
  B=1  Stop          -> stands completely still
  B=4  Attraction    -> moves toward nearby robots
            requires --att<I>  real in [1..5]  (attraction gain)
  B=5  Repulsion     -> moves away from nearby robots
            requires --rep<I>  real in [1..5]  (repulsion gain)
  B=6  Spiral        -> traces an expanding spiral
            requires --spd<I>  integer in [1..100]  (spiral speed)
  B=8  GoToColor     -> moves toward the nearest object of a target colour
            requires --vel<I>  fixed value 1.0  (speed)
            requires --clr<I>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (target colour)
  B=9  GoAwayColor   -> moves away from the nearest object of a target colour
            requires --vel<I>  fixed value 1.0  (speed)
            requires --clr<I>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (target colour)
  B=10  FlockCenter   -> moves toward the centre of the group
            requires --fc<I>  integer in [1..100]  (flocking gain)
  B=11  ZigZag        -> moves in a zigzag pattern
            requires --zfr<I>  integer in [1..100]  (zigzag frequency)

Every state also takes an LED colour:
* `--cle<I> L` : one of {0,4,5,6}  (0=off, 1=red, 2=green, 3=blue, 4=yellow, 5=magenta, 6=cyan)

## C. Transitions
* `--n<I> T` : number of transitions leaving state <I>, integer in [1..4].
  Up to 4 transitions may leave the same state, each with a different condition type.
* `--n<I>x<K> D` : destination, indexed among the OTHER states only.
  With 3 states, leaving state 1 the others are [0, 2]: D=0 targets state 0, D=1 targets state 2.
  With 2 states, leaving state 0 the only other state is state 1, so D must be 0.
* `--c<I>x<K> C` : condition type — use ONLY these ids
  C=0  floor under the robot is BLACK
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=1  floor under the robot is GRAY
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=2  floor under the robot is WHITE
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=3  number of neighbours ABOVE a threshold
            requires --p<I>x<K>  integer in [1..10]  (threshold: an INTEGER count of neighbours, not a probability)
            requires --w<I>x<K>  real in [0..20]  (steepness of the sigmoid)
  C=4  number of neighbours BELOW a threshold
            requires --p<I>x<K>  integer in [1..10]  (threshold: an INTEGER count of neighbours, not a probability)
            requires --w<I>x<K>  real in [0..20]  (steepness of the sigmoid)
  C=5  fires with a fixed probability at every step
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=7  the robot PERCEIVES a given colour with its camera
            requires --l<I>x<K>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (colour to look for)
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])

# 6. HARD RULES
1. Integers stay integers; real-valued parameters always carry a decimal point (1.0, not 1).
2. Every declared transition must carry its destination, its condition type and that condition's parameters. A missing parameter crashes the simulator.
3. `--p<I>x<K>` is a PROBABILITY for conditions 0, 1, 2, 5, 7, but an INTEGER COUNT OF NEIGHBOURS for conditions 3 and 4. Never write 0.8 where a neighbour count is expected.

# 7. YOU ARE THE EVOLUTIONARY OPERATOR
This is a multi-round conversation on one real ARGoS3 optimisation run. The numerical optimiser is restarted from scratch at the beginning of every round, so exploration and exploitation are entirely up to you.

1. SURVIVAL (elitism): copy the current best-ever machine verbatim in your answer so its lineage is not lost.
2. EXPLOITATION: spend about half of your machines on small edits of the best performers — shift a probability, a threshold or a gain by roughly ten percent, and keep the topology.
3. EXPLORATION: spend the rest on genuinely different topologies or module combinations, to escape local optima.
4. RICHNESS: do not let every state settle on a single transition of the same condition type. Include candidates with two to four transitions per state mixing different condition types.
